People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

Yeah, um… I came in because I keep blowing up at people over basically nothing. Like on operations calls, someone will ask a normal question and I just snap, and then I’m sitting there afterward thinking, why did I do that?

And it’s not just work. I’ve been doing it at home too, with my family, over little stuff that shouldn’t matter. I mean, I’ve managed people for years, I’m usually the guy who keeps things steady when everyone else is getting worked up… so this is, it’s not like me.

I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, down… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to separate that from just being worn out and frustrated with myself.

But lately it’s like it’s right there under the surface. Someone asks me something on an operations call—normal stuff, stuff I’ve handled a thousand times—and I just snap. Then I’m angry at myself afterward.\n\nAnd it’s happening at home too, with my family, over nothing. I’ve always been the steady one, you know? The one who keeps things moving, keeps people calm. So yeah… it’s happening a lot lately, enough that I came in because it doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had a tendency to be irritable or is this a new development for you?\n\nPatient: No, no… this is new. I mean, I’ve had bad days like anybody, sure—running operations, you get people calling about schedules, trucks, staffing, some manager who didn’t read the email… that stuff can wear on you. That’s what bothers me. I’ve always been more the steady one, the one who keeps things calm, so this doesn’t feel like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Yeah… anxious, I guess, but it’s more like I’m on alert all the time.

Like at work, any time I hear a forklift backing up with that beep-beep-beep, or one of the compressors kicks on, my whole body just tightens before I even think about it. I start looking around—where are the exits, who’s standing where, what could go wrong next. And it’s stupid because those sounds are normal in operations. I’ve heard them forever.

Even at home, the smoke alarm had that little low-battery chirp the other day, and I was halfway across the room before I even knew what I was doing. Then I’m standing there feeling ridiculous, because it’s just a chirp, but my body doesn’t seem to know that.

scares me is there are chunks I just can’t pull up, especially those seconds around the alarm and the shutdown, even though people keep telling me I did the right things.\n- key: S076\n  name: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually kind of why I’m sitting here, I guess.

I keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone will ask me something simple—something I’ve answered a hundred times—and I just snap. And then I’m sitting there afterward thinking, what the hell was that? Because that’s not how I handle people.

I used to be the one who smoothed things over, you know? I ran crews, handled drivers, warehouse issues, all that mess—there was always some fire to put out. But I could usually keep my tone steady.

Now it’s like I go from zero to angry before I even catch myself. It feels really out of character.

I don’t know if I’d say I’m a different person, like fundamentally, but… something’s off. I keep thinking, “This isn’t me.”

Yeah… the big thing was about three weeks ago. There was a valve failure at the facility, and somebody got hurt.

And then at home too, same thing. My family says something normal, little stuff, and I hear myself getting sharp before I even really decide to. It’s embarrassing, because I’ve handled crews and problems and all kinds of pressure without acting like this.

But I’ve never been the guy who blows up over nothing.\n\nLately I’m snapping on calls when someone just asks a normal question, and then at home too, with my family, over little things. That’s what bothers me. I’ve always been more the steady one, the one who keeps things calm, so this doesn’t feel like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, down… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to separate that from just being worn out and frustrated with myself.\n\nLike, I’ll get off a call after snapping at someone, and then I’m sitting there thinking about how I used to be the guy people trusted to keep things calm. Back when I first started managing shifts, my old supervisor used to say, “Don’t let the room see you sweat,” and I kind of built my whole style around that. So now when I don’t do that, it bothers me.\n\nBut sad most of the time? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess, but it’s more like I’m on alert all the time.\n\nLike at work, any time I hear a forklift backing up with that beep-beep-beep, or one of the compressors kicks on, my whole body just tightens before I even think about it. I start looking around—where are the exits, who’s standing where, what could go wrong next. And it’s stupid because those sounds are normal in operations. I’ve heard them forever.\n\nEven at home, the smoke alarm had that little low-battery chirp the other day, and I was halfway across the room before I even knew what I was doing. Then I’m standing there feeling ridiculous, because it’s just a chirp, but my body doesn’t seem to know that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or how you interact with people that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually kind of why I’m sitting here, I guess.\n\nI keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone will ask me something simple—something I’ve answered a hundred times—and I just snap. And then I’m sitting there afterward thinking, what the hell was that? Because that’s not how I handle people.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. It’s at home too, with my family. Little stuff that shouldn’t even register, and I’m reacting like it’s a huge problem. I used to be the one who smoothed things over, you know? I ran crews, handled drivers, warehouse issues, all that mess—there was always some fire to put out. But I could usually keep my tone steady.\n\nNow it’s like I go from zero to angry before I even catch myself. It feels really out of character.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still the same person you've always been, or has something fundamentally changed about who you are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m a different person, like fundamentally, but… something’s off. I keep thinking, “This isn’t me.”\n\nI’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. On operations calls, I’ll snap at somebody for asking a normal question, and then five minutes later I’m embarrassed because I’ve handled way worse than that for years. Same thing at home with my family—little stuff, and I react like it’s some major issue.\n\nI used to be pretty steady. Not perfect, but steady. So yeah, it feels like something changed, even if I don’t know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes, losses, or particularly stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the big thing was about three weeks ago. There was a valve failure at the facility, and somebody got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking through the whole setup, like who signed off on what, whether the maintenance schedule was right, whether we should’ve caught it sooner—and then I start thinking about totally unrelated stuff, like how that area always had bad lighting and I’d complained about it before, and… I don’t know.\n\nBut yeah. Since that happened, since the injury, things have been like this. About three weeks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these irritability episodes constantly throughout the day, or are they more sporadic?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every single minute, no. It comes in these bursts, I guess. Like I’ll be fine—or trying to be fine—and then someone asks me something on an operations call and I just snap. Over nothing.\n\nAnd then at home too, same thing. My family says something normal, little stuff, and I hear myself getting sharp before I even really decide to. It’s embarrassing, because I’ve handled crews and problems and all kinds of pressure without acting like this.\n\nSo it’s sporadic, but it’s happening enough that it feels like it’s taking over. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other traumatic events in your life besides the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I mean, not that I’d call traumatic, I guess. I’ve had stressful stuff, sure. Work stuff, family stuff, normal life, you know? When you’re in operations long enough, there are always close calls and stupid decisions people make, and you end up carrying it around a little.\n\nBut nothing that’s sticking in my head like this one. Or… I don’t know, maybe I’m not thinking of it the right way. When you say traumatic, do you mean like accidents, or like personal losses, or…? Because I wouldn’t want to make something sound bigger than it was.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not really, no… I mean, nobody close to me has died recently or anything like that. My family’s still around, thank God.

I start looking around—where are the exits, who’s standing where, what could go wrong next. And it’s stupid because those sounds are normal in operations. I’ve heard them forever.\n\nEven at home, the smoke alarm had that little low-battery chirp the other day, and I was halfway across the room before I even knew what I was doing. Then I’m standing there feeling ridiculous, because it’s just a chirp, but my body doesn’t seem to know that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or how you interact with people that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually kind of why I’m sitting here, I guess.\n\nI keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone will ask me something simple—something I’ve answered a hundred times—and I just snap. And then I’m sitting there afterward thinking, what the hell was that? I start looking around—where are the exits, who’s standing where, what could go wrong next. And it’s stupid because those sounds are normal in operations. I’ve heard them forever.\n\nEven at home, the smoke alarm had that little low-battery chirp the other day, and I was halfway across the room before I even knew what I was doing. Then I’m standing there feeling ridiculous, because it’s just a chirp, but my body doesn’t seem to know that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or how you interact with people that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually kind of why I’m sitting here, I guess.\n\nI keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone will ask me something simple—something I’ve answered a hundred times—and I just snap. And then I’m sitting there afterward thinking, what the hell was that? Because that’s not how I handle people.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. It’s at home too, with my family. Little stuff that shouldn’t even register, and I’m reacting like it’s a huge problem. I used to be the one who smoothed things over, you know? I ran crews, handled drivers, warehouse issues, all that mess—there was always some fire to put out. But I could usually keep my tone steady.\n\nNow it’s like I go from zero to angry before I even catch myself. It feels really out of character.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still the same person you've always been, or has something fundamentally changed about who you are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m a different person, like fundamentally, but… something’s off. I keep thinking, “This isn’t me.”\n\nI’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. On operations calls, I’ll snap at somebody for asking a normal question, and then five minutes later I’m embarrassed because I’ve handled way worse than that for years. Same thing at home with my family—little stuff, and I react like it’s some major issue.\n\nI used to be pretty steady. Not perfect, but steady. So yeah, it feels like something changed, even if I don’t know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes, losses, or particularly stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the big thing was about three weeks ago. There was a valve failure at the facility, and somebody got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking through the whole setup, like who signed off on what, whether the maintenance schedule was right, whether we should’ve caught it sooner—and then I start thinking about totally unrelated stuff, like how that area always had bad lighting and I’d complained about it before, and… I don’t know.\n\nBut yeah. Since that happened, since the injury, things have been like this. About three weeks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these irritability episodes constantly throughout the day, or are they more sporadic?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every single minute, no. My family says something normal, little stuff, and I hear myself getting sharp before I even really decide to. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other traumatic events in your life besides the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I mean, not that I’d call traumatic, I guess. Or… I don’t know, maybe I’m not thinking of it the right way. When you say traumatic, do you mean like accidents, or like personal losses, or…? Because I wouldn’t want to make something sound bigger than it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal losses recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, nobody close to me has died recently or anything like that. No… no, I wouldn’t say always. I mean, I’ve had a temper like anybody, and in operations you have to raise your voice sometimes just to be heard over the nonsense—sorry, that sounds like an excuse.

Like on operations calls, someone will ask a normal question and I just snap, and then I’m sitting there afterward thinking, why did I do that?\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I’ve been doing it at home too, with my family, over little stuff that shouldn’t matter. I mean, I’ve managed people for years, I’m usually the guy who keeps things steady when everyone else is getting worked up… so this is, it’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Is this irritability something you've been experiencing all the time lately?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, exactly… I mean, I’m not walking around yelling every second. But lately it’s like it’s right there under the surface. Someone asks me something on an operations call—normal stuff, stuff I’ve handled a thousand times—and I just snap. Then I’m angry at myself afterward.\n\nAnd it’s happening at home too, with my family, over nothing. I’ve always been the steady one, you know? The one who keeps things moving, keeps people calm. So yeah… it’s happening a lot lately, enough that I came in because it doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had a tendency to be irritable or is this a new development for you?\n\nPatient: No, no… this is new. I mean, I’ve had bad days like anybody, sure—running operations, you get people calling about schedules, trucks, staffing, some manager who didn’t read the email… that stuff can wear on you. But I’ve never been the guy who blows up over nothing.\n\nLately I’m snapping on calls when someone just asks a normal question, and then at home too, with my family, over little things. That’s what bothers me. I’ve always been more the steady one, the one who keeps things calm, so this doesn’t feel like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, down… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to separate that from just being worn out and frustrated with myself.\n\nLike, I’ll get off a call after snapping at someone, and then I’m sitting there thinking about how I used to be the guy people trusted to keep things calm. Back when I first started managing shifts, my old supervisor used to say, “Don’t let the room see you sweat,” and I kind of built my whole style around that. So now when I don’t do that, it bothers me.\n\nBut sad most of the time? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess, but it’s more like I’m on alert all the time.\n\nLike at work, any time I hear a forklift backing up with that beep-beep-beep, or one of the compressors kicks on, my whole body just tightens before I even think about it. I start looking around—where are the exits, who’s standing where, what could go wrong next. And it’s stupid because those sounds are normal in operations. I’ve heard them forever.\n\nEven at home, the smoke alarm had that little low-battery chirp the other day, and I was halfway across the room before I even knew what I was doing. Then I’m standing there feeling ridiculous, because it’s just a chirp, but my body doesn’t seem to know that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or how you interact with people that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually kind of why I’m sitting here, I guess.\n\nI keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone will ask me something simple—something I’ve answered a hundred times—and I just snap. And then I’m sitting there afterward thinking, what the hell was that? Because that’s not how I handle people.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. It’s at home too, with my family. Little stuff that shouldn’t even register, and I’m reacting like it’s a huge problem. I used to be the one who smoothed things over, you know? I ran crews, handled drivers, warehouse issues, all that mess—there was always some fire to put out. But I could usually keep my tone steady.\n\nNow it’s like I go from zero to angry before I even catch myself. It feels really out of character.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still the same person you've always been, or has something fundamentally changed about who you are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m a different person, like fundamentally, but… something’s off. I keep thinking, “This isn’t me.”\n\nI’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. On operations calls, I’ll snap at somebody for asking a normal question, and then five minutes later I’m embarrassed because I’ve handled way worse than that for years. Same thing at home with my family—little stuff, and I react like it’s some major issue.\n\nI used to be pretty steady. Not perfect, but steady. So yeah, it feels like something changed, even if I don’t know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes, losses, or particularly stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the big thing was about three weeks ago. There was a valve failure at the facility, and somebody got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking through the whole setup, like who signed off on what, whether the maintenance schedule was right, whether we should’ve caught it sooner—and then I start thinking about totally unrelated stuff, like how that area always had bad lighting and I’d complained about it before, and… I don’t know.\n\nBut yeah. Since that happened, since the injury, things have been like this. About three weeks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these irritability episodes constantly throughout the day, or are they more sporadic?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every single minute, no. It comes in these bursts, I guess. Like I’ll be fine—or trying to be fine—and then someone asks me something on an operations call and I just snap. Over nothing.\n\nAnd then at home too, same thing. My family says something normal, little stuff, and I hear myself getting sharp before I even really decide to. It’s embarrassing, because I’ve handled crews and problems and all kinds of pressure without acting like this.\n\nSo it’s sporadic, but it’s happening enough that it feels like it’s taking over. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other traumatic events in your life besides the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I mean, not that I’d call traumatic, I guess. I’ve had stressful stuff, sure. Work stuff, family stuff, normal life, you know? When you’re in operations long enough, there are always close calls and stupid decisions people make, and you end up carrying it around a little.\n\nBut nothing that’s sticking in my head like this one. Or… I don’t know, maybe I’m not thinking of it the right way. When you say traumatic, do you mean like accidents, or like personal losses, or…? Because I wouldn’t want to make something sound bigger than it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal losses recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, nobody close to me has died recently or anything like that. My family’s still around, thank God.\n\nThere are losses at work sometimes—people leaving, projects getting scrapped, budgets cut—and you feel those more than you think, especially when you’ve spent months trying to keep something from falling apart. But I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, significant personal loss… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty getting along with others or found it hard to maintain relationships due to your own behaviors or attitudes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not always, anyway. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people, sure. You don’t get to be a manager without butting heads sometimes—vendors, maintenance guys, corporate people who’ve never set foot on the floor but think a spreadsheet tells the whole story.\n\nBut relationships? I’ve generally been able to keep things steady, I think. I’m not saying I’m easy all the time. My wife would probably have her own list if you asked her—like how I get focused on work and forget I promised to fix something around the house. There was this whole thing with the garage door last year, because I said I’d handle it and then the parts sat there for weeks…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I wouldn’t say I’ve always had trouble getting along with people because of my attitude. Not like a lifelong pattern or anything that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had a tendency to react impulsively or aggressively in stressful situations, even before this recent change?\n\nPatient: No… no, I wouldn’t say always. I mean, I’ve had a temper like anybody, and in operations you have to raise your voice sometimes just to be heard over the nonsense—sorry, that sounds like an excuse.\n\nBut this is different. I’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone asks a normal question and I snap like they’re accusing me of something. And at home too, with my family, over stupid little things that don’t deserve it.\n\nThat’s not how I used to handle stress. I used to be the guy calming everybody down, or at least trying to keep the line moving. Now it’s like I’m already halfway angry before anything even happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And at home too, with my family, over stupid little things that don’t deserve it.\n\nThat’s not how I used to handle stress. I used to be the guy calming everybody down, or at least trying to keep the line moving. Now it’s like I’m already halfway angry before anything even happens.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your irritability episodes been getting better, worse, or staying about the same since they started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / general_or_operations_manager / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Irritability_Aggression\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing, snapping on operations calls and even at my family, and that is not how I’ve ever handled things.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wish I could say it was getting better, but… it’s still pretty bad. Maybe worse in the sense that I notice it everywhere now.

I’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like, on operations calls, someone asks something normal—just a normal question—and I snap at them like they’re coming after me. And then at home it happens too, with my family, over things that shouldn’t matter. It’s not how I’ve ever handled things before.

I keep thinking, “Okay, just keep your mouth shut next time,” but then the next time comes and I’m already irritated before I even know why. So… yeah, I don’t think it’s improving.

I’ve generally been able to keep things steady, I think. I’m not saying I’m easy all the time. My wife would probably have her own list if you asked her—like how I get focused on work and forget I promised to fix something around the house. There was this whole thing with the garage door last year, because I said I’d handle it and then the parts sat there for weeks…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I wouldn’t say I’ve always had trouble getting along with people because of my attitude. Not like a lifelong pattern or anything that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had a tendency to react impulsively or aggressively in stressful situations, even before this recent change?\n\nPatient: No… no, I wouldn’t say always. I mean, I’ve had a temper like anybody, and in operations you have to raise your voice sometimes just to be heard over the nonsense—sorry, that sounds like an excuse.\n\nBut this is different. I’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone asks a normal question and I snap like they’re accusing me of something. And at home too, with my family, over stupid little things that don’t deserve it.\n\nThat’s not how I used to handle stress. I used to be the guy calming everybody down, or at least trying to keep the line moving. Now it’s like I’m already halfway angry before anything even happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your irritability episodes been getting better, worse, or staying about the same since they started?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say it was getting better, but… it’s still pretty bad. Maybe worse in the sense that I notice it everywhere now.\n\nI’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like, on operations calls, someone asks something normal—just a normal question—and I snap at them like they’re coming after me. And then at home it happens too, with my family, over things that shouldn’t matter. It’s not how I’ve ever handled things before.\n\nI keep thinking, “Okay, just keep your mouth shut next time,” but then the next time comes and I’m already irritated before I even know why. So… yeah, I don’t think it’s improving.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the valve failure incident at the facility triggered these irritability episodes?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know how else to explain the timing of it. After that valve failure, it’s like something in me just got stuck on edge.

I keep blowing up at people over nothing now. On operations calls, someone asks a regular question and I snap like they’re challenging me, when they’re not. Since that happened, since the injury, things have been like this. But I could usually keep my tone steady.\n\nNow it’s like I go from zero to angry before I even catch myself. It feels really out of character.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still the same person you've always been, or has something fundamentally changed about who you are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m a different person, like fundamentally, but… something’s off. I keep thinking, “This isn’t me.”\n\nI’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. On operations calls, I’ll snap at somebody for asking a normal question, and then five minutes later I’m embarrassed because I’ve handled way worse than that for years. Same thing at home with my family—little stuff, and I react like it’s some major issue.\n\nI used to be pretty steady. Not perfect, but steady. So yeah, it feels like something changed, even if I don’t know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes, losses, or particularly stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the big thing was about three weeks ago. There was a valve failure at the facility, and somebody got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking through the whole setup, like who signed off on what, whether the maintenance schedule was right, whether we should’ve caught it sooner—and then I start thinking about totally unrelated stuff, like how that area always had bad lighting and I’d complained about it before, and… I don’t know.\n\nBut yeah. Since that happened, since the injury, things have been like this. About three weeks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these irritability episodes constantly throughout the day, or are they more sporadic?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every single minute, no. It comes in these bursts, I guess. Like I’ll be fine—or trying to be fine—and then someone asks me something on an operations call and I just snap. Over nothing.\n\nAnd then at home too, same thing. My family says something normal, little stuff, and I hear myself getting sharp before I even really decide to. It’s embarrassing, because I’ve handled crews and problems and all kinds of pressure without acting like this.\n\nSo it’s sporadic, but it’s happening enough that it feels like it’s taking over. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other traumatic events in your life besides the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I mean, not that I’d call traumatic, I guess. I’ve had stressful stuff, sure. Work stuff, family stuff, normal life, you know? When you’re in operations long enough, there are always close calls and stupid decisions people make, and you end up carrying it around a little.\n\nBut nothing that’s sticking in my head like this one. Or… I don’t know, maybe I’m not thinking of it the right way. When you say traumatic, do you mean like accidents, or like personal losses, or…? My family’s still around, thank God.\n\nThere are losses at work sometimes—people leaving, projects getting scrapped, budgets cut—and you feel those more than you think, especially when you’ve spent months trying to keep something from falling apart. I handled pressure all the time—breakdowns, staffing issues, corporate breathing down our necks, all of it. But this… this feels different. So yeah, I think that incident set something off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding anything related to your work or the facility where the valve failure happened since the incident occurred?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding. I mean, work is work, right? You can’t exactly just not answer the phone when you’re the operations guy.\n\nI still deal with the facility stuff, but… I guess I haven’t really thought about whether I’m steering away from certain parts of it. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just delegating because that’s what managers do, but then I get sidetracked with emails or some dumb staffing issue and—sorry, I’m not answering very cleanly.\n\nI’m not sure. I haven’t noticed myself avoiding it on purpose, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, “guilty” is a heavy word, right? I keep going over pieces of it, but then my mind jumps to the paperwork and the calls afterward, and who was supposed to sign off on what, and—\n\nSorry. I don’t know if I can cleanly say yes or no. I guess I’ve thought about whether I should’ve caught something sooner, but I don’t really know what that means yet. It’s all kind of tangled up.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're responsible for what happened during the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / general_or_operations_manager / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t… I don’t know. Responsible like legally, or responsible like I was the guy who should’ve seen it coming? Because those feel like different things in my head.

I mean, I was involved, sure, but there were a lot of moving parts. Maintenance logs, shift handoffs, vendors, the usual chain of approvals—half the time in that place you’re chasing one issue while three others are popping up. It’s like when my truck started making that noise last year, and I kept thinking it was the belt, but it turned out to be something totally different—sorry, that’s not really the same thing.

I guess I keep circling around it, but I can’t say plainly, “Yes, it was my fault.” I don’t know that. I also can’t seem to just let it sit as “not my responsibility” either. It’s… messy.

I mean, I’ve managed people for years, I’m usually the guy who keeps things steady when everyone else is getting worked up… so this is, it’s not like me.\n\nDoctor: Is this irritability something you've been experiencing all the time lately?\n\nPatient: Not all the time, exactly… I mean, I’m not walking around yelling every second. Back when I first started managing shifts, my old supervisor used to say, “Don’t let the room see you sweat,” and I kind of built my whole style around that. So now when I don’t do that, it bothers me.\n\nBut sad most of the time? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess, but it’s more like I’m on alert all the time.\n\nLike at work, any time I hear a forklift backing up with that beep-beep-beep, or one of the compressors kicks on, my whole body just tightens before I even think about it. I start looking around—where are the exits, who’s standing where, what could go wrong next. And it’s stupid because those sounds are normal in operations. I’ve heard them forever.\n\nEven at home, the smoke alarm had that little low-battery chirp the other day, and I was halfway across the room before I even knew what I was doing. That’s actually kind of why I’m sitting here, I guess.\n\nI keep blowing up at people over nothing. On operations calls, I’ll snap at somebody for asking a normal question, and then five minutes later I’m embarrassed because I’ve handled way worse than that for years. Same thing at home with my family—little stuff, and I react like it’s some major issue.\n\nI used to be pretty steady. Not perfect, but steady. So yeah, it feels like something changed, even if I don’t know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes, losses, or particularly stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the big thing was about three weeks ago. There was a valve failure at the facility, and somebody got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking through the whole setup, like who signed off on what, whether the maintenance schedule was right, whether we should’ve caught it sooner—and then I start thinking about totally unrelated stuff, like how that area always had bad lighting and I’d complained about it before, and… I don’t know.\n\nBut yeah. Since that happened, since the injury, things have been like this. About three weeks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these irritability episodes constantly throughout the day, or are they more sporadic?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every single minute, no. It comes in these bursts, I guess. Like I’ll be fine—or trying to be fine—and then someone asks me something on an operations call and I just snap. Over nothing.\n\nAnd then at home too, same thing. My family says something normal, little stuff, and I hear myself getting sharp before I even really decide to. It’s embarrassing, because I’ve handled crews and problems and all kinds of pressure without acting like this.\n\nSo it’s sporadic, but it’s happening enough that it feels like it’s taking over. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other traumatic events in your life besides the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I mean, not that I’d call traumatic, I guess. I’ve had stressful stuff, sure. Work stuff, family stuff, normal life, you know? When you’re in operations long enough, there are always close calls and stupid decisions people make, and you end up carrying it around a little.\n\nBut nothing that’s sticking in my head like this one. Or… I don’t know, maybe I’m not thinking of it the right way. When you say traumatic, do you mean like accidents, or like personal losses, or…? Because I wouldn’t want to make something sound bigger than it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal losses recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, nobody close to me has died recently or anything like that. My family’s still around, thank God.\n\nThere are losses at work sometimes—people leaving, projects getting scrapped, budgets cut—and you feel those more than you think, especially when you’ve spent months trying to keep something from falling apart. But I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, significant personal loss… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty getting along with others or found it hard to maintain relationships due to your own behaviors or attitudes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not always, anyway. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people, sure. You don’t get to be a manager without butting heads sometimes—vendors, maintenance guys, corporate people who’ve never set foot on the floor but think a spreadsheet tells the whole story.\n\nBut relationships? I’ve generally been able to keep things steady, I think. I’m not saying I’m easy all the time. My wife would probably have her own list if you asked her—like how I get focused on work and forget I promised to fix something around the house. There was this whole thing with the garage door last year, because I said I’d handle it and then the parts sat there for weeks…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I wouldn’t say I’ve always had trouble getting along with people because of my attitude. Not like a lifelong pattern or anything that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had a tendency to react impulsively or aggressively in stressful situations, even before this recent change?\n\nPatient: No… no, I wouldn’t say always. I’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone asks a normal question and I snap like they’re accusing me of something. And at home too, with my family, over stupid little things that don’t deserve it.\n\nThat’s not how I used to handle stress. I used to be the guy calming everybody down, or at least trying to keep the line moving. Now it’s like I’m already halfway angry before anything even happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your irritability episodes been getting better, worse, or staying about the same since they started?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say it was getting better, but… it’s still pretty bad. And then at home it happens too, with my family, over things that shouldn’t matter. It’s not how I’ve ever handled things before.\n\nI keep thinking, “Okay, just keep your mouth shut next time,” but then the next time comes and I’m already irritated before I even know why. So… yeah, I don’t think it’s improving.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the valve failure incident at the facility triggered these irritability episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Back when I first started managing shifts, my old supervisor used to say, “Don’t let the room see you sweat,” and I kind of built my whole style around that. So now when I don’t do that, it bothers me.\n\nBut sad most of the time? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious, I guess, but it’s more like I’m on alert all the time.\n\nLike at work, any time I hear a forklift backing up with that beep-beep-beep, or one of the compressors kicks on, my whole body just tightens before I even think about it. I start looking around—where are the exits, who’s standing where, what could go wrong next. And it’s stupid because those sounds are normal in operations. I’ve heard them forever.\n\nEven at home, the smoke alarm had that little low-battery chirp the other day, and I was halfway across the room before I even knew what I was doing. Then I’m standing there feeling ridiculous, because it’s just a chirp, but my body doesn’t seem to know that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or how you interact with people that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually kind of why I’m sitting here, I guess.\n\nI keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone will ask me something simple—something I’ve answered a hundred times—and I just snap. And then I’m sitting there afterward thinking, what the hell was that? Because that’s not how I handle people.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. It’s at home too, with my family. Little stuff that shouldn’t even register, and I’m reacting like it’s a huge problem. I used to be the one who smoothed things over, you know? I ran crews, handled drivers, warehouse issues, all that mess—there was always some fire to put out. But I could usually keep my tone steady.\n\nNow it’s like I go from zero to angry before I even catch myself. It feels really out of character.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still the same person you've always been, or has something fundamentally changed about who you are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m a different person, like fundamentally, but… something’s off. I keep thinking, “This isn’t me.”\n\nI’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. On operations calls, I’ll snap at somebody for asking a normal question, and then five minutes later I’m embarrassed because I’ve handled way worse than that for years. Same thing at home with my family—little stuff, and I react like it’s some major issue.\n\nI used to be pretty steady. Not perfect, but steady. So yeah, it feels like something changed, even if I don’t know what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes, losses, or particularly stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the big thing was about three weeks ago. There was a valve failure at the facility, and somebody got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking through the whole setup, like who signed off on what, whether the maintenance schedule was right, whether we should’ve caught it sooner—and then I start thinking about totally unrelated stuff, like how that area always had bad lighting and I’d complained about it before, and… I don’t know.\n\nBut yeah. Since that happened, since the injury, things have been like this. About three weeks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these irritability episodes constantly throughout the day, or are they more sporadic?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every single minute, no. It comes in these bursts, I guess. Like I’ll be fine—or trying to be fine—and then someone asks me something on an operations call and I just snap. Over nothing.\n\nAnd then at home too, same thing. My family says something normal, little stuff, and I hear myself getting sharp before I even really decide to. It’s embarrassing, because I’ve handled crews and problems and all kinds of pressure without acting like this.\n\nSo it’s sporadic, but it’s happening enough that it feels like it’s taking over. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other traumatic events in your life besides the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I mean, not that I’d call traumatic, I guess. I’ve had stressful stuff, sure. Work stuff, family stuff, normal life, you know? When you’re in operations long enough, there are always close calls and stupid decisions people make, and you end up carrying it around a little.\n\nBut nothing that’s sticking in my head like this one. Or… I don’t know, maybe I’m not thinking of it the right way. When you say traumatic, do you mean like accidents, or like personal losses, or…? Because I wouldn’t want to make something sound bigger than it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant personal losses recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, nobody close to me has died recently or anything like that. My family’s still around, thank God.\n\nThere are losses at work sometimes—people leaving, projects getting scrapped, budgets cut—and you feel those more than you think, especially when you’ve spent months trying to keep something from falling apart. But I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, significant personal loss… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty getting along with others or found it hard to maintain relationships due to your own behaviors or attitudes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not always, anyway. I mean, I’ve had disagreements with people, sure. You don’t get to be a manager without butting heads sometimes—vendors, maintenance guys, corporate people who’ve never set foot on the floor but think a spreadsheet tells the whole story.\n\nBut relationships? I’ve generally been able to keep things steady, I think. I’m not saying I’m easy all the time. My wife would probably have her own list if you asked her—like how I get focused on work and forget I promised to fix something around the house. There was this whole thing with the garage door last year, because I said I’d handle it and then the parts sat there for weeks…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I wouldn’t say I’ve always had trouble getting along with people because of my attitude. Not like a lifelong pattern or anything that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had a tendency to react impulsively or aggressively in stressful situations, even before this recent change?\n\nPatient: No… no, I wouldn’t say always. I mean, I’ve had a temper like anybody, and in operations you have to raise your voice sometimes just to be heard over the nonsense—sorry, that sounds like an excuse.\n\nBut this is different. I’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like on operations calls, someone asks a normal question and I snap like they’re accusing me of something. And at home too, with my family, over stupid little things that don’t deserve it.\n\nThat’s not how I used to handle stress. I used to be the guy calming everybody down, or at least trying to keep the line moving. Now it’s like I’m already halfway angry before anything even happens.\n\nDoctor: Have your irritability episodes been getting better, worse, or staying about the same since they started?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say it was getting better, but… it’s still pretty bad. Maybe worse in the sense that I notice it everywhere now.\n\nI’m here because I keep blowing up at people over nothing. Like, on operations calls, someone asks something normal—just a normal question—and I snap at them like they’re coming after me. And then at home it happens too, with my family, over things that shouldn’t matter. It’s not how I’ve ever handled things before.\n\nI keep thinking, “Okay, just keep your mouth shut next time,” but then the next time comes and I’m already irritated before I even know why. So… yeah, I don’t think it’s improving.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the valve failure incident at the facility triggered these irritability episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know how else to explain the timing of it. After that valve failure, it’s like something in me just got stuck on edge.\n\nI keep blowing up at people over nothing now. On operations calls, someone asks a regular question and I snap like they’re challenging me, when they’re not. And then I go home and do it with my family too, over dumb little stuff. My wife will ask me something simple and I hear myself answering like she did something wrong.\n\nAnd I wasn’t like that before. I handled pressure all the time—breakdowns, staffing issues, corporate breathing down our necks, all of it. But this… this feels different. So yeah, I think that incident set something off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding anything related to your work or the facility where the valve failure happened since the incident occurred?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it avoiding. I mean, work is work, right? You can’t exactly just not answer the phone when you’re the operations guy.\n\nI still deal with the facility stuff, but… I guess I haven’t really thought about whether I’m steering away from certain parts of it. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just delegating because that’s what managers do, but then I get sidetracked with emails or some dumb staffing issue and—sorry, I’m not answering very cleanly.\n\nI’m not sure. I haven’t noticed myself avoiding it on purpose, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, “guilty” is a heavy word, right? I keep going over pieces of it, but then my mind jumps to the paperwork and the calls afterward, and who was supposed to sign off on what, and—\n\nSorry. I don’t know if I can cleanly say yes or no. I guess I’ve thought about whether I should’ve caught something sooner, but I don’t really know what that means yet. It’s all kind of tangled up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're responsible for what happened during the valve failure incident at the facility?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t know. Responsible like legally, or responsible like I was the guy who should’ve seen it coming? Because those feel like different things in my head.\n\nI mean, I was involved, sure, but there were a lot of moving parts. Maintenance logs, shift handoffs, vendors, the usual chain of approvals—half the time in that place you’re chasing one issue while three others are popping up. It’s like when my truck started making that noise last year, and I kept thinking it was the belt, but it turned out to be something totally different—sorry, that’s not really the same thing.\n\nI guess I keep circling around it, but I can’t say plainly, “Yes, it was my fault.” I don’t know that. I also can’t seem to just let it sit as “not my responsibility” either. It’s… messy.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these episodes of irritability and snapping at people?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. Since the valve failure happened and that injury at the facility… that’s when it started getting bad.\n\nI mean, I’ve snapped before, everybody does, especially in operations—people miss a shift, a delivery’s late, some valve spec doesn’t match the paperwork, and you get heated. But this is different. Or it feels different. Sorry, you asked how long—yeah, about three weeks. Since that day.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of irritability and snapping at people lasted for more than a month?\n\nPatient: No, not more than a month. It’s been about three weeks… basically since the valve failure and the injury at the facility.\n\nI keep thinking it feels longer, like it’s been taking up so much space that the days all blur together, and then I’ll look at the calendar or remember which shift it happened on and—yeah. About three weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
